Tired? Low on energy? Well, I suppose you could say that, but it's not tired like I used to get tired. You know, when I was working at the school, by Friday afternoon I'd be absolutely exhausted. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. 

But this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, a nice sage green color, very comfortable, but you shouldn't get tired just sitting in it all day. I sleep alright, I think. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. But when I wake up, I don't feel refreshed. I don't feel that spring in my step like I used to. 

It's more like... like my limbs are made of lead. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's nice to meet you too, though I suppose I wish it were under different circumstances, you know? My daughter is the one who insisted I come down here, she kept calling and calling until I finally said I'd go. What brings me in... well, it's just that I don't have the push I used to have. It's hard to explain, really. I used to be so involved in things, you know? I was in the church choir for years, alto section, and we practiced every Wednesday evening and sang on Sundays. I loved it, I really did. And I used to quilt with a group of ladies from the neighborhood, we'd meet up and talk and sew, and I used to visit folks, bring them a casserole if they were sick or just sit and chat. \n\nBut lately, I just... I know I should be doing these things, I know they're waiting for me, but the thought of getting up, getting dressed, going out, talking to everyone... it just feels like a mountain I can't climb. I just sit there with the TV murmuring, and the whole day just slips right by me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that a minute. It didn't happen all at once, you know, like a light switch turning off or something like that. It’s been more of a... a slow thing, just creeping up on me little by little. I’d say it’s been building up since I retired. \n\nI used to work down at the local school office—just helping out, making sure the papers were sorted and the desks were wiped down, running messages to the classrooms, things like that. It wasn't anything fancy, but I liked seeing the little ones in the halls and keeping busy. But I retired from there a little over two years ago. My daughter threw me a nice little dinner and everything, baked a ham. And at first, I thought, well, this is grand, I can finally rest my feet and just take it easy for a spell. \n\nBut then the resting just... it just sort of took over, if you see what I mean. Ever since I packed up my things at that school office, a little over two years back, this... this lack of push just started building. First I stopped going to the extra choir practices because I thought I'd earned a break, then I stopped going on Sundays altogether, and before I knew it, two whole years had gone by and I'm just sitting in that armchair. I mean, I suppose you could say it started then, though I didn't see it coming. Like I was saying, I worked at the school office, and they had a little party for me. My daughter, she baked a ham, and she put those little pineapple rings on it with the cherries in the middle, just the way I like it. And she brought over a nice potato salad, the kind with the egg mixed in, not too much mustard. I woke up the next Monday, and I didn't have to put on my sensible shoes or pack my little lunch in the blue cooler bag. I didn't have to walk down to the corner to catch the bus. I just sat down in the armchair, thinking I'd just read the paper for a bit, maybe look at the coupons, you know? But then the paper was done, and I just kept sitting. And then Tuesday came, and I sat some more. It just felt like the string that was pulling me along just snapped, right there after the ham dinner. I kept thinking, \"I'll go to choir next week,\" or \"I'll call Mrs. Higgins tomorrow,\" but tomorrow just kept turning into today, and I was still in the chair. So, yes, doctor, it was the retirement, but it wasn't like a sudden illness, it was just... the stopping. The stopping just never stopped.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you felt down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed... I don't know if I'd use that word exactly. My sister, Clara, now she was depressed back in the eighties. She wouldn't get out of bed, wouldn't wash her hair, just cried and cried until her husband finally had to take her to a special clinic two towns over. It was a terrible time, just terrible. I remember going over there to help with the kids, making them macaroni and cheese because they wouldn't eat anything else, and she'd just be sobbing in the bedroom. \n\nBut me? I don't cry. I haven't cried since my husband passed, Lord rest his soul, and that was fifteen years ago this coming May. I don't feel sad, not really. It's not like a heavy, weeping kind of feeling. It's more like... like a flatness, if that makes sense. Like soda that's been left out on the counter too long and all the fizz is gone. \n\nI don't sit in my chair feeling sorry for myself or thinking dark thoughts. I just sit there. I watch the dust motes floating in the sunlight that comes through the front window—the window with the lace curtains I bought at Sears before they closed down the one at the mall—and I just watch them float. I don't feel happy, I don't feel sad, I just... am. So, depressed? No, I don't think so. Just empty, I suppose. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you felt this flat, low-motivation way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, pretty much every single day. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, this whole thing has been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago. It hasn't skipped a day, no sir. I'd wake up on a Tuesday, and it was flat. I'd wake up on a Saturday, and it was flat. \n\nI used to be up with the sun, you know. Up at five-thirty, making my instant coffee—Folgers, the crystal kind, because I never had the patience for one of those fancy drip machines that take ten minutes to brew—and I'd be out the door. But now? It's every day, Monday through Sunday. The mail carrier comes, I hear the brass slot on the front door clatter—it's a heavy slot, probably needs a bit of oil, honestly, it squeaks terribly—and I just sit there and think... well, I'll get the mail tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same flat feeling. \n\nIt just started right after that retirement, a little over two years back, and it just piled up, day after day, week after week. It doesn't take a day off, not even on holidays. Easter, Thanksgiving, it doesn't matter. My daughter brought over a plate of turkey this past year, and I just sat there looking at it. So, yes, every single day since the school job ended, it's just been this steady building of... well, of nothing. Just the same empty flat feeling, from the minute I open my eyes till the minute I close them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this flat and unmotivated continuously since your retirement, without any stretch of a couple months where you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no breaks. Not a single one, doctor. Not a week, not a month, certainly not a couple of months. Like I was saying, it just started and it stayed. I keep thinking back to last spring, when the daffodils came up. I planted those bulbs myself, oh, must have been ten years ago now, the yellow ones with the orange centers, right along the front walkway where the sun hits them in the afternoon. And usually, when they pop up, I get a little thrill, you know? I'd go out with my shears and cut a few for a vase on the kitchen table. But last spring, they bloomed, and they looked just as pretty as ever, but I just looked at them through the glass and didn't feel a thing. I didn't even go outside to smell them. And then the summer came, and the heat set in, the kind of heat that makes the tar on the street soft, and I still just sat there in the air conditioning. And then autumn... well, you get the picture. There hasn't been a time where I woke up and thought, \"Oh, I feel like myself again today! I think I'll bake a pie or call my friend Shirley.\" It's just been one long, unbroken stretch of this... this flatness, ever since the retirement. It's like a gray blanket got thrown over everything and nobody ever came to pull it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, no, doctor, no trouble with that at all. In fact, sleeping is about the only thing I do seem to have any real talent for these days. It's funny you ask, because my neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lives in the blue house next door, the one with the overgrown rhododendrons that keep scraping against my gutters—she's always complaining about her insomnia. She tells me she's up at two, three in the morning, pacing the floor, drinking warm milk, watching those infomercials where they try to sell you a blender that can chop up a whole pineapple. She says her mind just races. \n\nBut me? I don't have that problem. Like I said, my mind doesn't race, it just sort of... sits there. So when it gets dark, usually right after the evening news with David Muir—I've always liked him, he has such a kind face, though I miss Peter Jennings, he had that wonderful voice—anyway, right after the news, I just turn off the television and go to bed. And I fall asleep right away. My head hits the pillow, that goose-down pillow I've had for years, probably should replace it but it's just got the right squish to it, you know? And I'm out. \n\nI sleep all through the night. I don't wake up to use the restroom, I don't wake up because I'm hot or cold, I just sleep a solid eight, sometimes nine hours. And then I wake up, and there's that flat feeling again, waiting for me like an old dog at the foot of the bed. So, no, my sleep is perfectly fine. I just wish I had something to do with myself once I actually woke up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has changed?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Well... no, I haven't really noticed any change there. I mean, I still eat my three meals a day, just like I always have. It's funny, you'd think with everything feeling so flat and gray, I wouldn't want to eat, but my stomach still rumbles right on schedule. \n\nI usually start with a piece of toast in the morning—just plain white bread, usually Wonder Bread, because it toasts up so nice and crisp, you know? And I put a little pat of butter on it, the salted kind, because the unsalted just tastes like wax to me. I used to make eggs, sunny-side up, with a little sprinkle of black pepper, but the pan is just so heavy to wash these days, so toast it is. And for lunch, I usually have a bowl of soup. Campbell's, mostly. The Chicken Noodle is classic, of course, but sometimes I go for the Cream of Mushroom, especially if it's chilly out. I heat it up on the stove—never the microwave, it always heats unevenly in there, leaves cold spots in the middle—and I have a few saltines with it. \n\nAnd dinner is just whatever is easy. A frozen dinner, maybe a Salisbury steak or a piece of baked chicken. I don't really taste it much, to be honest. It's not like I sit there and think, \"Oh, this is delicious,\" the way I used to when I'd make my pot roast with the little pearl onions and the carrots that get so soft they melt in your mouth. Now, eating is just something I do because the clock says it's time to do it. It's noon, so I eat soup. It's six o'clock, so I heat up a dinner. But the amount I'm eating, the actual appetite itself? No, that hasn't changed. I'm not eating any more or any less than I did before I retired. It's just... routine, I suppose. Just going through the motions of chewing and swallowing without any of the joy that used to come with a good meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Oh, no, I wouldn't use that word. No, I don't feel worthless. I know I have value, doctor. I worked hard my whole life, you know? Started when I was just a girl, helping out, and I've always paid my own way, always kept a clean house. Even now, with everything feeling so gray and heavy, I still make sure the trash goes out on Tuesdays. The garbage men come so early, usually around 5:30 in the morning, and you can hear the big truck grinding its gears all the way down Elm Street. If you don't have the bins out the night before, they just drive right past, and then you're stuck with it for a whole week. And you know how melon rinds get in the summer if they sit too long. \n\nSo no, I don't feel worthless. I just feel... empty. Like a pitcher that somebody poured all the water out of and just left sitting on the counter. It's still a perfectly good pitcher, it's not broken, it's not trash... it's just not doing anything. My sister, Brenda, she used to say that when people retire, they lose their purpose, and maybe that's what this is. But worthless? No. I still have my dignity, I still have my home. I just don't have any... any spark left to do anything with them. But I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space, nothing like that. It's just this awful flatness.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Well... yes, doctor, I suppose I have. That's actually what brings me in, really. I just don't have the push I used to have for the things I used to do. It's like the starter motor is broken, you know? Like, take my quilting. I used to quilt all the time. I'd sit by the big window in the front room, the one that gets the good afternoon light, and I'd piece together these intricate patterns—Log Cabin, Flying Geese, Double Wedding Ring. I'd spend hours picking out the right calico fabrics at JoAnn's, matching the colors just so. But now... my sewing machine is just sitting there under its plastic cover. It's a Singer, a good heavy one from the seventies, not those plastic things they make now, but I haven't turned it on in months. \n\nAnd choir, too. I've been singing alto in the First Methodist choir for, oh, it has to be thirty years. We rehearse on Thursday nights, 7:00 PM sharp, and Pastor Miller is always so particular about us being on time. I used to love the harmonies, the way all our voices would blend together, especially on the old hymns like 'How Great Thou Art' or 'In the Garden.' But lately, I just... I don't go. I know they're expecting me, I know they're probably wondering where I am, maybe even a little short on altos, but I just can't bring myself to put my coat on and walk out the door. \n\nSame with visiting folks. I used to bake a nice lemon pound cake—the secret is a little bit of sour cream in the batter, makes it so moist—and go over to Brenda's or Mrs. Gable's just to chat and have coffee. But now, most days, I just sit at home in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, the kind that rocks, in a sort of tan microfiber that doesn't show stains. I just sit there with the TV on low. Not even watching it, really. Just having it on so the house isn't so quiet. The Price is Right will be on, and I'll see the colors moving on the screen, but I don't care if they win the showcase showdown or not. I just sit there, knowing I should be doing something, knowing people are expecting me, but that push... it's just gone. Completely gone.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been times when you could still enjoy things, even a little?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? Well... I suppose I try to think back on that, and it's hard to say. I mean, you say two years, but really, this whole thing, this... this heavy, gray feeling where the starter motor is just broken, like I said, it hasn't just been two years exactly. It’s been building, you see. It’s been building up ever since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago. \n\nI was the attendance secretary at Lincoln Elementary, you know. Oh, I knew every child’s name, and their parents’ names, and who was out with the chickenpox and who was just trying to skip a spelling test. I had my desk organized just so, with the pink slips for tardiness in the top left drawer and the blue slips for early dismissal right next to them. I had a whole system. And when I retired, they threw me a lovely party in the staff lounge. They had a sheet cake from Costco—half vanilla, half chocolate, with the buttercream frosting, not that whipped stuff that melts if the room gets too warm. They gave me a beautiful glass vase, too, heavy crystal, the kind that catches the light if you put it on the windowsill. \n\nBut after that... after the cake was eaten and the vase was put on the shelf, that’s when it started building. At first, I thought I was just tired, you know? Just catching my breath after working all those years. I thought, \"Oh, I’ll just rest a bit.\" But the resting never stopped. The quiet just got louder. So, to answer your question about enjoying things during this time... it’s just been a slow fade. Well... I don't know if I'd say I've had trouble concentrating, exactly. I don't care if they guess the price of the Rice-A-Roni or the jet ski. \n\nAnd I suppose I still read my Bible sometimes, usually just a Psalm or two before I go to sleep. I’d promise to bring in a batch of hats by Tuesday, and then Tuesday would come and go, and the yarn would just sit in the basket next to my chair, untouched. I just couldn't keep up with what I promised. It got to the point where I felt so guilty, but even the guilt wasn't enough to make me pick up the knitting needles. \n\nNow, my friends from church, like Martha and Susan—Susan is the one who always brings those wonderful lemon bars to the potlucks, with the powdered sugar on top—they've started calling me more often. They come by to check on me, bringing casseroles and asking if I need anything from the grocery store. They never used to do that. They know something's not right, even if I try to tell them I'm just resting. So, yes, doctor, I suppose this heavy feeling has been interfering with the things I used to do, quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Low on energy? Well, I suppose you could say that, but it's not tired like I used to get tired. You know, when I was working at the school, by Friday afternoon I'd be absolutely exhausted. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. \n\nBut this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I keep it on the nightstand next to my little lamp with the fringed shade. I can read a Psalm just fine, read the words right off the page, so my mind isn't wandering off or anything like that. It's just that there's not much I'm trying to focus my mind on these days. So, no, I haven't really noticed any trouble with my concentration, doctor. Just... just the lack of wanting to do anything that needs it.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and motivation been interfering with your ability to take care of things you need to do in daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, taking care of things... I suppose it has, yes. I mean, it's not like I'm not eating or bathing—I still manage to make my little egg salad sandwich, and I always make sure to shower in the mornings, using that lavender soap my niece bought me for Christmas two years ago. It comes in a purple box with a little ribbon tied around it, so pretty you almost don't want to open it. But as for the other things, the things outside the house... yes, it's been getting in the way. \n\nYou see, I used to be very involved at my church, St. Jude's on Elm Street. I was the choir director for... oh, it must have been twelve years. We had practice every Wednesday evening at 7:00 PM sharp, and I was always there early to set out the sheet music and make sure the piano was dusted. We sang all the traditional hymns, you know, \"How Great Thou Art\" and \"Amazing Grace,\" none of that modern guitar nonsense they do at some of the other parishes now. But lately... lately I just haven't had the energy for it. I had to step back from leading the choir. I just couldn't bring myself to go, and I'd sit in my recliner thinking about how they were all waiting for me, and I just couldn't move. \n\nAnd it's not just the choir. I used to help out a lot with the projects down at the senior center, too. We were knitting little caps for the premature babies at the hospital—I was using a soft yellow yarn, very gentle on the skin. But I stopped going to that, too. I’d promise to bring in a batch of hats by Tuesday, and then Tuesday would come and go, and the yarn would just sit in the basket next to my chair, untouched. I just couldn't keep up with what I promised. It got to the point where I felt so guilty, but even the guilt wasn't enough to make me pick up the knitting needles. \n\nNow, my friends from church, like Martha and Susan—Susan is the one who always brings those wonderful lemon bars to the potlucks, with the powdered sugar on top—they've started calling me more often. They come by to check on me, bringing casseroles and asking if I need anything from the grocery store. They never used to do that. They know something's not right, even if I try to tell them I'm just resting. So, yes, doctor, I suppose this heavy feeling has been interfering with the things I used to do, quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Low on energy? Well, I suppose you could say that, but it's not tired like I used to get tired. You know, when I was working at the school, by Friday afternoon I'd be absolutely exhausted. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. \n\nBut this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, a nice sage green color, very comfortable, but you shouldn't get tired just sitting in it all day. I sleep alright, I think. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. But when I wake up, I don't feel refreshed. I don't feel that spring in my step like I used to. \n\nIt's more like... like my limbs are made of lead. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Two weeks? Well... I couldn't rightly point to any specific two weeks on the calendar, doctor. You see, when you're just sitting in the house all day, the days just sort of blend into one another. I have this calendar hanging on the refrigerator—it’s one of those free ones they give you down at the pharmacy on Main Street, the one with the pictures of the national parks. October has a beautiful picture of the Grand Canyon, all those red and orange rocks, just lovely. I used to take a little red marker and put an 'X' through every day before I went to bed, just to keep track of things. But lately, I haven't even been doing that. The red marker is just sitting there in the little ceramic mug I keep my pens in. 

So I don't really keep track of the weeks anymore. But as for it getting noticeably worse... no, I wouldn't say it ever dipped down worse than usual. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster, you know, going up and down and making your stomach drop. It’s just been one long, flat stretch. It actually reminds me of when my late husband Arthur and I drove out through the Midwest in our old Ford station wagon—it was powder blue with that fake wood paneling on the side, a real boat of a car. We drove through Nebraska, and it was just flat, flat, flat, as far as the eye could see, for hours on end. You'd look out the window and nothing ever changed. 

That’s exactly how this feeling is. It’s just the same heavy, flat feeling every single day, from the time I wake up until I turn off my little fringed lamp at night. It doesn't get heavier, but it doesn't lift, either. So, no, I haven't had any particular two weeks where it took a sudden turn for the worse. Just the same steady flatness, day in and day out.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF34.1, F43.2, F32.0, F32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's nice to meet you too, though I suppose I wish it were under different circumstances, you know? My daughter is the one who insisted I come down here, she kept calling and calling until I finally said I'd go. What brings me in... well, it's just that I don't have the push I used to have. It's hard to explain, really. I used to be so involved in things, you know? I was in the church choir for years, alto section, and we practiced every Wednesday evening and sang on Sundays. I loved it, I really did. And I used to quilt with a group of ladies from the neighborhood, we'd meet up and talk and sew, and I used to visit folks, bring them a casserole if they were sick or just sit and chat. \n\nBut lately, I just... I don't know, I just can't seem to find the energy or the desire for any of it anymore. Most days, I just sit at home in my armchair in the living room. I keep the TV on, but just on low, just for the background noise, really, not even watching it half the time. And the thing is, I know people are expecting me. The choir director called twice last month, and Mrs. Higgins from the quilting group even stopped by, but I just couldn't bring myself to answer the door. I just sat there listening to her knock. I know I should be doing these things, I know they're waiting for me, but the thought of getting up, getting dressed, going out, talking to everyone... it just feels like a mountain I can't climb. I just sit there with the TV murmuring, and the whole day just slips right by me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think on that a minute. It didn't happen all at once, you know, like a light switch turning off or something like that. It’s been more of a... a slow thing, just creeping up on me little by little. I’d say it’s been building up since I retired. \n\nI used to work down at the local school office—just helping out, making sure the papers were sorted and the desks were wiped down, running messages to the classrooms, things like that. It wasn't anything fancy, but I liked seeing the little ones in the halls and keeping busy. But I retired from there a little over two years ago. My daughter threw me a nice little dinner and everything, baked a ham. And at first, I thought, well, this is grand, I can finally rest my feet and just take it easy for a spell. \n\nBut then the resting just... it just sort of took over, if you see what I mean. Ever since I packed up my things at that school office, a little over two years back, this... this lack of push just started building. First I stopped going to the extra choir practices because I thought I'd earned a break, then I stopped going on Sundays altogether, and before I knew it, two whole years had gone by and I'm just sitting in that armchair. So, yes, a little over two years, right after the school job ended, that's when the days just started melting together.\n\nDoctor: Did this change in you start after your retirement?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly, right after the retirement. I mean, I suppose you could say it started then, though I didn't see it coming. Like I was saying, I worked at the school office, and they had a little party for me. My daughter, she baked a ham, and she put those little pineapple rings on it with the cherries in the middle, just the way I like it. And she brought over a nice potato salad, the kind with the egg mixed in, not too much mustard. We sat at my dining table, the one with the claw feet that used to belong to my mother, and she gave me a card signed by all the teachers. \n\nAnd after that, I just... I woke up the next Monday, and I didn't have to put on my sensible shoes or pack my little lunch in the blue cooler bag. I didn't have to walk down to the corner to catch the bus. I just sat down in the armchair, thinking I'd just read the paper for a bit, maybe look at the coupons, you know? But then the paper was done, and I just kept sitting. And then Tuesday came, and I sat some more. It just felt like the string that was pulling me along just snapped, right there after the ham dinner. I kept thinking, \"I'll go to choir next week,\" or \"I'll call Mrs. Higgins tomorrow,\" but tomorrow just kept turning into today, and I was still in the chair. So, yes, doctor, it was the retirement, but it wasn't like a sudden illness, it was just... the stopping. The stopping just never stopped.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you felt down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, depressed... I don't know if I'd use that word exactly. My sister, Clara, now she was depressed back in the eighties. She wouldn't get out of bed, wouldn't wash her hair, just cried and cried until her husband finally had to take her to a special clinic two towns over. It was a terrible time, just terrible. I remember going over there to help with the kids, making them macaroni and cheese because they wouldn't eat anything else, and she'd just be sobbing in the bedroom. \n\nBut me? I don't cry. I haven't cried since my husband passed, Lord rest his soul, and that was fifteen years ago this coming May. I don't feel sad, not really. It's not like a heavy, weeping kind of feeling. It's more like... like a flatness, if that makes sense. Like soda that's been left out on the counter too long and all the fizz is gone. \n\nI don't sit in my chair feeling sorry for myself or thinking dark thoughts. I just sit there. I watch the dust motes floating in the sunlight that comes through the front window—the window with the lace curtains I bought at Sears before they closed down the one at the mall—and I just watch them float. I don't feel happy, I don't feel sad, I just... am. So, depressed? No, I don't think so. Just empty, I suppose. Just completely empty.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have you felt this flat, low-motivation way nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yes, doctor, pretty much every single day. Like I was telling you just a minute ago, this whole thing has been building since I retired from the school office, a little over two years ago. It hasn't skipped a day, no sir. I'd wake up on a Tuesday, and it was flat. I'd wake up on a Saturday, and it was flat. \n\nI used to be up with the sun, you know. Up at five-thirty, making my instant coffee—Folgers, the crystal kind, because I never had the patience for one of those fancy drip machines that take ten minutes to brew—and I'd be out the door. But now? It's every day, Monday through Sunday. The mail carrier comes, I hear the brass slot on the front door clatter—it's a heavy slot, probably needs a bit of oil, honestly, it squeaks terribly—and I just sit there and think... well, I'll get the mail tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same flat feeling. \n\nIt just started right after that retirement, a little over two years back, and it just piled up, day after day, week after week. It doesn't take a day off, not even on holidays. Easter, Thanksgiving, it doesn't matter. My daughter brought over a plate of turkey this past year, and I just sat there looking at it. So, yes, every single day since the school job ended, it's just been this steady building of... well, of nothing. Just the same empty flat feeling, from the minute I open my eyes till the minute I close them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this flat and unmotivated continuously since your retirement, without any stretch of a couple months where you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no breaks. Not a single one, doctor. Not a week, not a month, certainly not a couple of months. Like I was saying, it just started and it stayed. I keep thinking back to last spring, when the daffodils came up. I planted those bulbs myself, oh, must have been ten years ago now, the yellow ones with the orange centers, right along the front walkway where the sun hits them in the afternoon. And usually, when they pop up, I get a little thrill, you know? I'd go out with my shears and cut a few for a vase on the kitchen table. But last spring, they bloomed, and they looked just as pretty as ever, but I just looked at them through the glass and didn't feel a thing. I didn't even go outside to smell them. And then the summer came, and the heat set in, the kind of heat that makes the tar on the street soft, and I still just sat there in the air conditioning. And then autumn... well, you get the picture. There hasn't been a time where I woke up and thought, \"Oh, I feel like myself again today! I think I'll bake a pie or call my friend Shirley.\" It's just been one long, unbroken stretch of this... this flatness, ever since the retirement. It's like a gray blanket got thrown over everything and nobody ever came to pull it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, no, doctor, no trouble with that at all. In fact, sleeping is about the only thing I do seem to have any real talent for these days. It's funny you ask, because my neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lives in the blue house next door, the one with the overgrown rhododendrons that keep scraping against my gutters—she's always complaining about her insomnia. She tells me she's up at two, three in the morning, pacing the floor, drinking warm milk, watching those infomercials where they try to sell you a blender that can chop up a whole pineapple. She says her mind just races. \n\nBut me? I don't have that problem. Like I said, my mind doesn't race, it just sort of... sits there. So when it gets dark, usually right after the evening news with David Muir—I've always liked him, he has such a kind face, though I miss Peter Jennings, he had that wonderful voice—anyway, right after the news, I just turn off the television and go to bed. And I fall asleep right away. My head hits the pillow, that goose-down pillow I've had for years, probably should replace it but it's just got the right squish to it, you know? And I'm out. \n\nI sleep all through the night. I don't wake up to use the restroom, I don't wake up because I'm hot or cold, I just sleep a solid eight, sometimes nine hours. And then I wake up, and there's that flat feeling again, waiting for me like an old dog at the foot of the bed. So, no, my sleep is perfectly fine. I just wish I had something to do with myself once I actually woke up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has changed?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Well... no, I haven't really noticed any change there. I mean, I still eat my three meals a day, just like I always have. It's funny, you'd think with everything feeling so flat and gray, I wouldn't want to eat, but my stomach still rumbles right on schedule. \n\nI usually start with a piece of toast in the morning—just plain white bread, usually Wonder Bread, because it toasts up so nice and crisp, you know? And I put a little pat of butter on it, the salted kind, because the unsalted just tastes like wax to me. I used to make eggs, sunny-side up, with a little sprinkle of black pepper, but the pan is just so heavy to wash these days, so toast it is. And for lunch, I usually have a bowl of soup. Campbell's, mostly. The Chicken Noodle is classic, of course, but sometimes I go for the Cream of Mushroom, especially if it's chilly out. I heat it up on the stove—never the microwave, it always heats unevenly in there, leaves cold spots in the middle—and I have a few saltines with it. \n\nAnd dinner is just whatever is easy. A frozen dinner, maybe a Salisbury steak or a piece of baked chicken. I don't really taste it much, to be honest. It's not like I sit there and think, \"Oh, this is delicious,\" the way I used to when I'd make my pot roast with the little pearl onions and the carrots that get so soft they melt in your mouth. Now, eating is just something I do because the clock says it's time to do it. It's noon, so I eat soup. It's six o'clock, so I heat up a dinner. But the amount I'm eating, the actual appetite itself? No, that hasn't changed. I'm not eating any more or any less than I did before I retired. It's just... routine, I suppose. Just going through the motions of chewing and swallowing without any of the joy that used to come with a good meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Oh, no, I wouldn't use that word. No, I don't feel worthless. I know I have value, doctor. I worked hard my whole life, you know? Started when I was just a girl, helping out, and I've always paid my own way, always kept a clean house. Even now, with everything feeling so gray and heavy, I still make sure the trash goes out on Tuesdays. The garbage men come so early, usually around 5:30 in the morning, and you can hear the big truck grinding its gears all the way down Elm Street. If you don't have the bins out the night before, they just drive right past, and then you're stuck with it for a whole week. And you know how melon rinds get in the summer if they sit too long. \n\nSo no, I don't feel worthless. I just feel... empty. Like a pitcher that somebody poured all the water out of and just left sitting on the counter. It's still a perfectly good pitcher, it's not broken, it's not trash... it's just not doing anything. My sister, Brenda, she used to say that when people retire, they lose their purpose, and maybe that's what this is. But worthless? No. I still have my dignity, I still have my home. I just don't have any... any spark left to do anything with them. But I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space, nothing like that. It's just this awful flatness.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Well... yes, doctor, I suppose I have. That's actually what brings me in, really. I just don't have the push I used to have for the things I used to do. It's like the starter motor is broken, you know? Like, take my quilting. I used to quilt all the time. I'd sit by the big window in the front room, the one that gets the good afternoon light, and I'd piece together these intricate patterns—Log Cabin, Flying Geese, Double Wedding Ring. I'd spend hours picking out the right calico fabrics at JoAnn's, matching the colors just so. But now... my sewing machine is just sitting there under its plastic cover. It's a Singer, a good heavy one from the seventies, not those plastic things they make now, but I haven't turned it on in months. \n\nAnd choir, too. I've been singing alto in the First Methodist choir for, oh, it has to be thirty years. We rehearse on Thursday nights, 7:00 PM sharp, and Pastor Miller is always so particular about us being on time. I used to love the harmonies, the way all our voices would blend together, especially on the old hymns like 'How Great Thou Art' or 'In the Garden.' But lately, I just... I don't go. I know they're expecting me, I know they're probably wondering where I am, maybe even a little short on altos, but I just can't bring myself to put my coat on and walk out the door. \n\nSame with visiting folks. I used to bake a nice lemon pound cake—the secret is a little bit of sour cream in the batter, makes it so moist—and go over to Brenda's or Mrs. Gable's just to chat and have coffee. But now, most days, I just sit at home in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, the kind that rocks, in a sort of tan microfiber that doesn't show stains. I just sit there with the TV on low. Not even watching it, really. Just having it on so the house isn't so quiet. The Price is Right will be on, and I'll see the colors moving on the screen, but I don't care if they win the showcase showdown or not. I just sit there, knowing I should be doing something, knowing people are expecting me, but that push... it's just gone. Completely gone.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been times when you could still enjoy things, even a little?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? Well... I suppose I try to think back on that, and it's hard to say. I mean, you say two years, but really, this whole thing, this... this heavy, gray feeling where the starter motor is just broken, like I said, it hasn't just been two years exactly. It’s been building, you see. It’s been building up ever since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago. \n\nI was the attendance secretary at Lincoln Elementary, you know. Oh, I knew every child’s name, and their parents’ names, and who was out with the chickenpox and who was just trying to skip a spelling test. I had my desk organized just so, with the pink slips for tardiness in the top left drawer and the blue slips for early dismissal right next to them. I had a whole system. And when I retired, they threw me a lovely party in the staff lounge. They had a sheet cake from Costco—half vanilla, half chocolate, with the buttercream frosting, not that whipped stuff that melts if the room gets too warm. They gave me a beautiful glass vase, too, heavy crystal, the kind that catches the light if you put it on the windowsill. \n\nBut after that... after the cake was eaten and the vase was put on the shelf, that’s when it started building. At first, I thought I was just tired, you know? Just catching my breath after working all those years. I thought, \"Oh, I’ll just rest a bit.\" But the resting never stopped. The quiet just got louder. So, to answer your question about enjoying things during this time... it’s just been a slow fade. Like a photograph left out in the sun too long, where the colors just sort of bleach out day by day. Since I left that school office, it’s just been creeping up on me, this... this nothingness. So, enjoy things? I don't know. Maybe right at the beginning, a little, before the gray really settled in, but it's been building for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Right after you retired, before this flat feeling really set in, was there a time when you still felt like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, doctor, it really has been building right since I walked out of that school office, which was a little over two years ago now. You ask if I felt like my usual self right after, and... well, maybe for just a handful of days? I remember waking up that very first Monday after my party—the one with the nice cake I told you about—and I didn't have to set my alarm clock. It’s a little wind-up one, brass on the top, loud enough to wake the dead. Usually I’d be up at 5:15 AM, brewing my coffee and making a little egg salad sandwich for my lunch, always with a dash of paprika in the mayonnaise. \n\nThat first week, I thought, well, this is just a nice vacation. I felt okay then, I suppose. I even cleaned out the hall closet and folded all my bath towels so the blue ones were on the bottom and the white ones on top, making sure the tags were all tucked inside so it looked neat. But that didn't last. Once the closet was clean, there was nothing else to do. So there wasn't some long stretch of time where I was my old self, no. Like I said, it’s been building up ever since that retirement started two years ago. It just started quiet, you know, like dust settling in a room nobody uses anymore, and then it just kept piling up until the flat feeling took over completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Well... I don't know if I'd say I've had trouble concentrating, exactly. I mean, it's hard to say what trouble concentrating really looks like when you're just sitting in the recliner all day, isn't it? It's not like when I was at the school, where I had to keep track of Mrs. Higgins' third-grade class going on a field trip to the dairy farm, and make sure all twenty-two permission slips were signed and filed in the manila folder with the green label. That took concentration, making sure little Timmy didn't get on the bus without his inhaler. \n\nBut now... I don't really have anything to concentrate *on*. Like I said, I'll have the TV on, maybe The Price is Right or sometimes Let's Make a Deal—though I never did care for Wayne Brady as much as Monty Hall, rest his soul, Monty just had such a nice way with the contestants, didn't he? Always so polite. Anyway, I'll have the TV on, and I'm not really paying attention to it, but it's not because I *can't* concentrate. It's just because I don't care to. I don't care if they guess the price of the Rice-A-Roni or the jet ski. \n\nAnd I suppose I still read my Bible sometimes, usually just a Psalm or two before I go to sleep. I keep it on the nightstand next to my little lamp with the fringed shade. I can read a Psalm just fine, read the words right off the page, so my mind isn't wandering off or anything like that. It's just that there's not much I'm trying to focus my mind on these days. So, no, I haven't really noticed any trouble with my concentration, doctor. Just... just the lack of wanting to do anything that needs it.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and motivation been interfering with your ability to take care of things you need to do in daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, taking care of things... I suppose it has, yes. I mean, it's not like I'm not eating or bathing—I still manage to make my little egg salad sandwich, and I always make sure to shower in the mornings, using that lavender soap my niece bought me for Christmas two years ago. It comes in a purple box with a little ribbon tied around it, so pretty you almost don't want to open it. But as for the other things, the things outside the house... yes, it's been getting in the way. \n\nYou see, I used to be very involved at my church, St. Jude's on Elm Street. I was the choir director for... oh, it must have been twelve years. We had practice every Wednesday evening at 7:00 PM sharp, and I was always there early to set out the sheet music and make sure the piano was dusted. We sang all the traditional hymns, you know, \"How Great Thou Art\" and \"Amazing Grace,\" none of that modern guitar nonsense they do at some of the other parishes now. But lately... lately I just haven't had the energy for it. I had to step back from leading the choir. I just couldn't bring myself to go, and I'd sit in my recliner thinking about how they were all waiting for me, and I just couldn't move. \n\nAnd it's not just the choir. I used to help out a lot with the projects down at the senior center, too. We were knitting little caps for the premature babies at the hospital—I was using a soft yellow yarn, very gentle on the skin. But I stopped going to that, too. I’d promise to bring in a batch of hats by Tuesday, and then Tuesday would come and go, and the yarn would just sit in the basket next to my chair, untouched. I just couldn't keep up with what I promised. It got to the point where I felt so guilty, but even the guilt wasn't enough to make me pick up the knitting needles. \n\nNow, my friends from church, like Martha and Susan—Susan is the one who always brings those wonderful lemon bars to the potlucks, with the powdered sugar on top—they've started calling me more often. They come by to check on me, bringing casseroles and asking if I need anything from the grocery store. They never used to do that. They know something's not right, even if I try to tell them I'm just resting. So, yes, doctor, I suppose this heavy feeling has been interfering with the things I used to do, quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Low on energy? Well, I suppose you could say that, but it's not tired like I used to get tired. You know, when I was working at the school, by Friday afternoon I'd be absolutely exhausted. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. \n\nBut this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, a nice sage green color, very comfortable, but you shouldn't get tired just sitting in it all day. I sleep alright, I think. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. But when I wake up, I don't feel refreshed. I don't feel that spring in my step like I used to. \n\nIt's more like... like my limbs are made of lead. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the flatness and loss of interest were noticeably worse than usual and present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Well... I couldn't rightly point to any specific two weeks on the calendar, doctor. You see, when you're just sitting in the house all day, the days just sort of blend into one another. I have this calendar hanging on the refrigerator—it’s one of those free ones they give you down at the pharmacy on Main Street, the one with the pictures of the national parks. October has a beautiful picture of the Grand Canyon, all those red and orange rocks, just lovely. I used to take a little red marker and put an 'X' through every day before I went to bed, just to keep track of things. But lately, I haven't even been doing that. The red marker is just sitting there in the little ceramic mug I keep my pens in. \n\nSo I don't really keep track of the weeks anymore. But as for it getting noticeably worse... no, I wouldn't say it ever dipped down worse than usual. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster, you know, going up and down and making your stomach drop. It’s just been one long, flat stretch. It actually reminds me of when my late husband Arthur and I drove out through the Midwest in our old Ford station wagon—it was powder blue with that fake wood paneling on the side, a real boat of a car. We drove through Nebraska, and it was just flat, flat, flat, as far as the eye could see, for hours on end. You'd look out the window and nothing ever changed. \n\nThat’s exactly how this feeling is. It’s just the same heavy, flat feeling every single day, from the time I wake up until I turn off my little fringed lamp at night. It doesn't get heavier, but it doesn't lift, either. So, no, I haven't had any particular two weeks where it took a sudden turn for the worse. Just the same steady flatness, day in and day out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Up at five-thirty, making my instant coffee—Folgers, the crystal kind, because I never had the patience for one of those fancy drip machines that take ten minutes to brew—and I'd be out the door. But now? It's every day, Monday through Sunday. The mail carrier comes, I hear the brass slot on the front door clatter—it's a heavy slot, probably needs a bit of oil, honestly, it squeaks terribly—and I just sit there and think... well, I'll get the mail tomorrow. And then tomorrow comes, and it's the exact same flat feeling. \n\nIt just started right after that retirement, a little over two years back, and it just piled up, day after day, week after week. It doesn't take a day off, not even on holidays. Easter, Thanksgiving, it doesn't matter. My daughter brought over a plate of turkey this past year, and I just sat there looking at it. So, yes, every single day since the school job ended, it's just been this steady building of... well, of nothing. Just the same empty flat feeling, from the minute I open my eyes till the minute I close them.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this flat and unmotivated continuously since your retirement, without any stretch of a couple months where you felt back to your usual self?\n\nPatient: No, no breaks. Not a single one, doctor. Not a week, not a month, certainly not a couple of months. Like I was saying, it just started and it stayed. I keep thinking back to last spring, when the daffodils came up. I planted those bulbs myself, oh, must have been ten years ago now, the yellow ones with the orange centers, right along the front walkway where the sun hits them in the afternoon. And usually, when they pop up, I get a little thrill, you know? I'd go out with my shears and cut a few for a vase on the kitchen table. But last spring, they bloomed, and they looked just as pretty as ever, but I just looked at them through the glass and didn't feel a thing. I didn't even go outside to smell them. And then the summer came, and the heat set in, the kind of heat that makes the tar on the street soft, and I still just sat there in the air conditioning. And then autumn... well, you get the picture. There hasn't been a time where I woke up and thought, \"Oh, I feel like myself again today! I think I'll bake a pie or call my friend Shirley.\" It's just been one long, unbroken stretch of this... this flatness, ever since the retirement. It's like a gray blanket got thrown over everything and nobody ever came to pull it off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Oh, no, doctor, no trouble with that at all. In fact, sleeping is about the only thing I do seem to have any real talent for these days. It's funny you ask, because my neighbor, Mrs. Gable—she lives in the blue house next door, the one with the overgrown rhododendrons that keep scraping against my gutters—she's always complaining about her insomnia. She tells me she's up at two, three in the morning, pacing the floor, drinking warm milk, watching those infomercials where they try to sell you a blender that can chop up a whole pineapple. She says her mind just races. \n\nBut me? I don't have that problem. Like I said, my mind doesn't race, it just sort of... sits there. So when it gets dark, usually right after the evening news with David Muir—I've always liked him, he has such a kind face, though I miss Peter Jennings, he had that wonderful voice—anyway, right after the news, I just turn off the television and go to bed. And I fall asleep right away. My head hits the pillow, that goose-down pillow I've had for years, probably should replace it but it's just got the right squish to it, you know? And I'm out. \n\nI sleep all through the night. I don't wake up to use the restroom, I don't wake up because I'm hot or cold, I just sleep a solid eight, sometimes nine hours. And then I wake up, and there's that flat feeling again, waiting for me like an old dog at the foot of the bed. So, no, my sleep is perfectly fine. I just wish I had something to do with myself once I actually woke up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your appetite has changed?\n\nPatient: Appetite? Well... no, I haven't really noticed any change there. I mean, I still eat my three meals a day, just like I always have. It's funny, you'd think with everything feeling so flat and gray, I wouldn't want to eat, but my stomach still rumbles right on schedule. \n\nI usually start with a piece of toast in the morning—just plain white bread, usually Wonder Bread, because it toasts up so nice and crisp, you know? And I put a little pat of butter on it, the salted kind, because the unsalted just tastes like wax to me. I used to make eggs, sunny-side up, with a little sprinkle of black pepper, but the pan is just so heavy to wash these days, so toast it is. And for lunch, I usually have a bowl of soup. Campbell's, mostly. The Chicken Noodle is classic, of course, but sometimes I go for the Cream of Mushroom, especially if it's chilly out. I heat it up on the stove—never the microwave, it always heats unevenly in there, leaves cold spots in the middle—and I have a few saltines with it. \n\nAnd dinner is just whatever is easy. A frozen dinner, maybe a Salisbury steak or a piece of baked chicken. I don't really taste it much, to be honest. It's not like I sit there and think, \"Oh, this is delicious,\" the way I used to when I'd make my pot roast with the little pearl onions and the carrots that get so soft they melt in your mouth. Now, eating is just something I do because the clock says it's time to do it. It's noon, so I eat soup. It's six o'clock, so I heat up a dinner. But the amount I'm eating, the actual appetite itself? No, that hasn't changed. I'm not eating any more or any less than I did before I retired. It's just... routine, I suppose. Just going through the motions of chewing and swallowing without any of the joy that used to come with a good meal.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Oh, no, I wouldn't use that word. No, I don't feel worthless. I know I have value, doctor. I worked hard my whole life, you know? Started when I was just a girl, helping out, and I've always paid my own way, always kept a clean house. Even now, with everything feeling so gray and heavy, I still make sure the trash goes out on Tuesdays. The garbage men come so early, usually around 5:30 in the morning, and you can hear the big truck grinding its gears all the way down Elm Street. If you don't have the bins out the night before, they just drive right past, and then you're stuck with it for a whole week. And you know how melon rinds get in the summer if they sit too long. \n\nSo no, I don't feel worthless. I just feel... empty. Like a pitcher that somebody poured all the water out of and just left sitting on the counter. It's still a perfectly good pitcher, it's not broken, it's not trash... it's just not doing anything. My sister, Brenda, she used to say that when people retire, they lose their purpose, and maybe that's what this is. But worthless? No. I still have my dignity, I still have my home. I just don't have any... any spark left to do anything with them. But I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space, nothing like that. It's just this awful flatness.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? Well... yes, doctor, I suppose I have. That's actually what brings me in, really. I just don't have the push I used to have for the things I used to do. It's like the starter motor is broken, you know? Like, take my quilting. I used to quilt all the time. I'd sit by the big window in the front room, the one that gets the good afternoon light, and I'd piece together these intricate patterns—Log Cabin, Flying Geese, Double Wedding Ring. I'd spend hours picking out the right calico fabrics at JoAnn's, matching the colors just so. But now... my sewing machine is just sitting there under its plastic cover. It's a Singer, a good heavy one from the seventies, not those plastic things they make now, but I haven't turned it on in months. \n\nAnd choir, too. I've been singing alto in the First Methodist choir for, oh, it has to be thirty years. We rehearse on Thursday nights, 7:00 PM sharp, and Pastor Miller is always so particular about us being on time. I used to love the harmonies, the way all our voices would blend together, especially on the old hymns like 'How Great Thou Art' or 'In the Garden.' But lately, I just... I don't go. I know they're expecting me, I know they're probably wondering where I am, maybe even a little short on altos, but I just can't bring myself to put my coat on and walk out the door. \n\nSame with visiting folks. I used to bake a nice lemon pound cake—the secret is a little bit of sour cream in the batter, makes it so moist—and go over to Brenda's or Mrs. Gable's just to chat and have coffee. But now, most days, I just sit at home in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, the kind that rocks, in a sort of tan microfiber that doesn't show stains. I just sit there with the TV on low. Not even watching it, really. Just having it on so the house isn't so quiet. The Price is Right will be on, and I'll see the colors moving on the screen, but I don't care if they win the showcase showdown or not. I just sit there, knowing I should be doing something, knowing people are expecting me, but that push... it's just gone. Completely gone.\n\nDoctor: During these past two years, have there been times when you could still enjoy things, even a little?\n\nPatient: Enjoy things? Well... I suppose I try to think back on that, and it's hard to say. I mean, you say two years, but really, this whole thing, this... this heavy, gray feeling where the starter motor is just broken, like I said, it hasn't just been two years exactly. It’s been building, you see. It’s been building up ever since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago. \n\nI was the attendance secretary at Lincoln Elementary, you know. Oh, I knew every child’s name, and their parents’ names, and who was out with the chickenpox and who was just trying to skip a spelling test. I had my desk organized just so, with the pink slips for tardiness in the top left drawer and the blue slips for early dismissal right next to them. I had a whole system. And when I retired, they threw me a lovely party in the staff lounge. They had a sheet cake from Costco—half vanilla, half chocolate, with the buttercream frosting, not that whipped stuff that melts if the room gets too warm. They gave me a beautiful glass vase, too, heavy crystal, the kind that catches the light if you put it on the windowsill. \n\nBut after that... after the cake was eaten and the vase was put on the shelf, that’s when it started building. At first, I thought I was just tired, you know? Just catching my breath after working all those years. I thought, \"Oh, I’ll just rest a bit.\" But the resting never stopped. The quiet just got louder. So, to answer your question about enjoying things during this time... it’s just been a slow fade. Like a photograph left out in the sun too long, where the colors just sort of bleach out day by day. Since I left that school office, it’s just been creeping up on me, this... this nothingness. So, enjoy things? I don't know. Maybe right at the beginning, a little, before the gray really settled in, but it's been building for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Right after you retired, before this flat feeling really set in, was there a time when you still felt like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, doctor, it really has been building right since I walked out of that school office, which was a little over two years ago now. You ask if I felt like my usual self right after, and... well, maybe for just a handful of days? I remember waking up that very first Monday after my party—the one with the nice cake I told you about—and I didn't have to set my alarm clock. It’s a little wind-up one, brass on the top, loud enough to wake the dead. Usually I’d be up at 5:15 AM, brewing my coffee and making a little egg salad sandwich for my lunch, always with a dash of paprika in the mayonnaise. \n\nThat first week, I thought, well, this is just a nice vacation. I felt okay then, I suppose. I even cleaned out the hall closet and folded all my bath towels so the blue ones were on the bottom and the white ones on top, making sure the tags were all tucked inside so it looked neat. But that didn't last. Once the closet was clean, there was nothing else to do. So there wasn't some long stretch of time where I was my old self, no. Like I said, it’s been building up ever since that retirement started two years ago. It just started quiet, you know, like dust settling in a room nobody uses anymore, and then it just kept piling up until the flat feeling took over completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Well... I don't know if I'd say I've had trouble concentrating, exactly. I mean, it's hard to say what trouble concentrating really looks like when you're just sitting in the recliner all day, isn't it? It's not like when I was at the school, where I had to keep track of Mrs. Higgins' third-grade class going on a field trip to the dairy farm, and make sure all twenty-two permission slips were signed and filed in the manila folder with the green label. That took concentration, making sure little Timmy didn't get on the bus without his inhaler. \n\nBut now... I don't really have anything to concentrate *on*. Like I said, I'll have the TV on, maybe The Price is Right or sometimes Let's Make a Deal—though I never did care for Wayne Brady as much as Monty Hall, rest his soul, Monty just had such a nice way with the contestants, didn't he? Always so polite. Anyway, I'll have the TV on, and I'm not really paying attention to it, but it's not because I *can't* concentrate. It's just because I don't care to. I don't care if they guess the price of the Rice-A-Roni or the jet ski. \n\nAnd I suppose I still read my Bible sometimes, usually just a Psalm or two before I go to sleep. I keep it on the nightstand next to my little lamp with the fringed shade. I can read a Psalm just fine, read the words right off the page, so my mind isn't wandering off or anything like that. It's just that there's not much I'm trying to focus my mind on these days. So, no, I haven't really noticed any trouble with my concentration, doctor. Just... just the lack of wanting to do anything that needs it.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and motivation been interfering with your ability to take care of things you need to do in daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, taking care of things... I suppose it has, yes. I mean, it's not like I'm not eating or bathing—I still manage to make my little egg salad sandwich, and I always make sure to shower in the mornings, using that lavender soap my niece bought me for Christmas two years ago. It comes in a purple box with a little ribbon tied around it, so pretty you almost don't want to open it. But as for the other things, the things outside the house... yes, it's been getting in the way. \n\nYou see, I used to be very involved at my church, St. Jude's on Elm Street. I was the choir director for... oh, it must have been twelve years. We had practice every Wednesday evening at 7:00 PM sharp, and I was always there early to set out the sheet music and make sure the piano was dusted. We sang all the traditional hymns, you know, \"How Great Thou Art\" and \"Amazing Grace,\" none of that modern guitar nonsense they do at some of the other parishes now. But lately... lately I just haven't had the energy for it. I had to step back from leading the choir. I just couldn't bring myself to go, and I'd sit in my recliner thinking about how they were all waiting for me, and I just couldn't move. \n\nAnd it's not just the choir. I used to help out a lot with the projects down at the senior center, too. We were knitting little caps for the premature babies at the hospital—I was using a soft yellow yarn, very gentle on the skin. But I stopped going to that, too. I’d promise to bring in a batch of hats by Tuesday, and then Tuesday would come and go, and the yarn would just sit in the basket next to my chair, untouched. I just couldn't keep up with what I promised. It got to the point where I felt so guilty, but even the guilt wasn't enough to make me pick up the knitting needles. \n\nNow, my friends from church, like Martha and Susan—Susan is the one who always brings those wonderful lemon bars to the potlucks, with the powdered sugar on top—they've started calling me more often. They come by to check on me, bringing casseroles and asking if I need anything from the grocery store. They never used to do that. They know something's not right, even if I try to tell them I'm just resting. So, yes, doctor, I suppose this heavy feeling has been interfering with the things I used to do, quite a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling tired or low on energy?\n\nPatient: Tired? Low on energy? Well, I suppose you could say that, but it's not tired like I used to get tired. You know, when I was working at the school, by Friday afternoon I'd be absolutely exhausted. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. \n\nBut this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, a nice sage green color, very comfortable, but you shouldn't get tired just sitting in it all day. I sleep alright, I think. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. But when I wake up, I don't feel refreshed. I don't feel that spring in my step like I used to. \n\nIt's more like... like my limbs are made of lead. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the flatness and loss of interest were noticeably worse than usual and present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Well... I couldn't rightly point to any specific two weeks on the calendar, doctor. You see, when you're just sitting in the house all day, the days just sort of blend into one another. I have this calendar hanging on the refrigerator—it’s one of those free ones they give you down at the pharmacy on Main Street, the one with the pictures of the national parks. October has a beautiful picture of the Grand Canyon, all those red and orange rocks, just lovely. I used to take a little red marker and put an 'X' through every day before I went to bed, just to keep track of things. But lately, I haven't even been doing that. The red marker is just sitting there in the little ceramic mug I keep my pens in. \n\nSo I don't really keep track of the weeks anymore. But as for it getting noticeably worse... no, I wouldn't say it ever dipped down worse than usual. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster, you know, going up and down and making your stomach drop. It’s just been one long, flat stretch. It actually reminds me of when my late husband Arthur and I drove out through the Midwest in our old Ford station wagon—it was powder blue with that fake wood paneling on the side, a real boat of a car. We drove through Nebraska, and it was just flat, flat, flat, as far as the eye could see, for hours on end. You'd look out the window and nothing ever changed. \n\nThat’s exactly how this feeling is. It’s just the same heavy, flat feeling every single day, from the time I wake up until I turn off my little fringed lamp at night. It doesn't get heavier, but it doesn't lift, either. So, no, I haven't had any particular two weeks where it took a sudden turn for the worse. Just the same steady flatness, day in and day out.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yes, doctor. Very much so. It hasn't gone away at all, even though the retirement is well in the past now. Honestly... well, this whole thing, this heavy, flat feeling, it's been building since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. 

I remember my last day so clearly. It was a Friday in late spring, and the principal, Mr. Henderson—such a tall man, he always wore those sweater vests with the little argyle patterns on the front—he brought in a sheet cake from the bakery down on 4th Street. It was vanilla with that whipped frosting, not the heavy buttercream, because he knew my stomach can't handle too much rich butter. They gave me a lovely little plaque, brass with my name engraved on it, thanking me for all my years keeping that front desk running and answering those ringing phones. I packed up my things, including that little ceramic mug I keep my pens in, put it all in a cardboard box, and I thought... well, I thought, now I’m going to have all this time to do my knitting and my church work and tend to my hydrangeas. I have the blue ones out front, you know, and you have to put coffee grounds in the soil to keep them that pretty color, otherwise they turn pink. 

But... instead of doing all those things, I just sort of came home, set that cardboard box on the kitchen counter, and sat down in my chair. And it’s just been building ever since that very afternoon. A little over two years of just... sinking deeper into it. At first, I told myself I was just taking a few days to rest after all those years of working, but then the resting just never stopped, did it? The days turned into weeks, and the weeks into months, and here we are, two whole years later, and I’m still just feeling so terribly flat. It didn't happen all at once, like a light switch turning off. It just sort of crept up on me, building and building, day by day, since the moment I walked out of that school office for the last time.

My daughter, she baked a ham, and she put those little pineapple rings on it with the cherries in the middle, just the way I like it. And she brought over a nice potato salad, the kind with the egg mixed in, not too much mustard.
