I mean, you say two years, but really, this whole thing, this... this heavy, gray feeling where the starter motor is just broken, like I said, it hasn't just been two years exactly. It’s been building, you see. It’s been building up ever since I retired from the school office, and that was a little over two years ago. \n\nI was the attendance secretary at Lincoln Elementary, you know. Oh, I knew every child’s name, and their parents’ names, and who was out with the chickenpox and who was just trying to skip a spelling test. I had my desk organized just so, with the pink slips for tardiness in the top left drawer and the blue slips for early dismissal right next to them. I had a whole system. And when I retired, they threw me a lovely party in the staff lounge. They had a sheet cake from Costco—half vanilla, half chocolate, with the buttercream frosting, not that whipped stuff that melts if the room gets too warm. They gave me a beautiful glass vase, too, heavy crystal, the kind that catches the light if you put it on the windowsill. \n\nBut after that... after the cake was eaten and the vase was put on the shelf, that’s when it started building. At first, I thought I was just tired, you know? Just catching my breath after working all those years. I thought, \"Oh, I’ll just rest a bit.\" But the resting never stopped. The quiet just got louder. So, to answer your question about enjoying things during this time... it’s just been a slow fade. Like a photograph left out in the sun too long, where the colors just sort of bleach out day by day. Since I left that school office, it’s just been creeping up on me, this... this nothingness. So, enjoy things? I don't know. Maybe right at the beginning, a little, before the gray really settled in, but it's been building for a while now.\n\nDoctor: Right after you retired, before this flat feeling really set in, was there a time when you still felt like your usual self?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, doctor, it really has been building right since I walked out of that school office, which was a little over two years ago now. You ask if I felt like my usual self right after, and... well, maybe for just a handful of days? I remember waking up that very first Monday after my party—the one with the nice cake I told you about—and I didn't have to set my alarm clock. It’s a little wind-up one, brass on the top, loud enough to wake the dead. Usually I’d be up at 5:15 AM, brewing my coffee and making a little egg salad sandwich for my lunch, always with a dash of paprika in the mayonnaise. \n\nThat first week, I thought, well, this is just a nice vacation. I felt okay then, I suppose. I even cleaned out the hall closet and folded all my bath towels so the blue ones were on the bottom and the white ones on top, making sure the tags were all tucked inside so it looked neat. But that didn't last. Once the closet was clean, there was nothing else to do. So there wasn't some long stretch of time where I was my old self, no. Like I said, it’s been building up ever since that retirement started two years ago. It just started quiet, you know, like dust settling in a room nobody uses anymore, and then it just kept piling up until the flat feeling took over completely.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: Concentrating? Well... I don't know if I'd say I've had trouble concentrating, exactly. I mean, it's hard to say what trouble concentrating really looks like when you're just sitting in the recliner all day, isn't it? It's not like when I was at the school, where I had to keep track of Mrs. Higgins' third-grade class going on a field trip to the dairy farm, and make sure all twenty-two permission slips were signed and filed in the manila folder with the green label. That took concentration, making sure little Timmy didn't get on the bus without his inhaler. \n\nBut now... I don't really have anything to concentrate *on*. Like I said, I'll have the TV on, maybe The Price is Right or sometimes Let's Make a Deal—though I never did care for Wayne Brady as much as Monty Hall, rest his soul, Monty just had such a nice way with the contestants, didn't he? Always so polite. Anyway, I'll have the TV on, and I'm not really paying attention to it, but it's not because I *can't* concentrate. It's just because I don't care to. I don't care if they guess the price of the Rice-A-Roni or the jet ski. \n\nAnd I suppose I still read my Bible sometimes, usually just a Psalm or two before I go to sleep. I keep it on the nightstand next to my little lamp with the fringed shade. I can read a Psalm just fine, read the words right off the page, so my mind isn't wandering off or anything like that. It's just that there's not much I'm trying to focus my mind on these days. So, no, I haven't really noticed any trouble with my concentration, doctor. Just... just the lack of wanting to do anything that needs it.\n\nDoctor: Has this loss of interest and motivation been interfering with your ability to take care of things you need to do in daily life?\n\nPatient: Well, taking care of things... I suppose it has, yes. I mean, it's not like I'm not eating or bathing—I still manage to make my little egg salad sandwich, and I always make sure to shower in the mornings, using that lavender soap my niece bought me for Christmas two years ago. It comes in a purple box with a little ribbon tied around it, so pretty you almost don't want to open it. But as for the other things, the things outside the house... yes, it's been getting in the way. \n\nYou see, I used to be very involved at my church, St. Jude's on Elm Street. I was the choir director for... oh, it must have been twelve years. We had practice every Wednesday evening at 7:00 PM sharp, and I was always there early to set out the sheet music and make sure the piano was dusted. We sang all the traditional hymns, you know, \"How Great Thou Art\" and \"Amazing Grace,\" none of that modern guitar nonsense they do at some of the other parishes now. I just couldn't bring myself to go, and I'd sit in my recliner thinking about how they were all waiting for me, and I just couldn't move. \n\nAnd it's not just the choir. I used to help out a lot with the projects down at the senior center, too. We were knitting little caps for the premature babies at the hospital—I was using a soft yellow yarn, very gentle on the skin. But I stopped going to that, too. I’d promise to bring in a batch of hats by Tuesday, and then Tuesday would come and go, and the yarn would just sit in the basket next to my chair, untouched. I just couldn't keep up with what I promised. It got to the point where I felt so guilty, but even the guilt wasn't enough to make me pick up the knitting needles. \n\nNow, my friends from church, like Martha and Susan—Susan is the one who always brings those wonderful lemon bars to the potlucks, with the powdered sugar on top—they've started calling me more often. They come by to check on me, bringing casseroles and asking if I need anything from the grocery store. They never used to do that. They know something's not right, even if I try to tell them I'm just resting. My feet would ache from standing on that hard linoleum floor all day, and my head would be spinning from all the phone calls and the children running in and out. That was a good tired, though. You'd come home, take your shoes off—I had these sensible navy blue pumps, Dr. Scholl's, very good for the arches—and you'd sit down with a cup of tea, maybe Earl Grey with just a splash of milk, and you'd feel like you earned your rest. \n\nBut this... this isn't like that. I'm not doing anything to *be* tired, am I? Like I said, I'm just sitting in my recliner. It's a La-Z-Boy, a nice sage green color, very comfortable, but you shouldn't get tired just sitting in it all day. I sleep alright, I think. I put my head on the pillow—I use a down alternative because the real feathers always poke through the casing and scratch my cheek—and I sleep through the night. But when I wake up, I don't feel refreshed. I don't feel that spring in my step like I used to. \n\nIt's more like... like my limbs are made of lead. Just getting up to make that egg salad sandwich feels like I'm dragging weights behind me. So I wouldn't say it's exactly that I'm tired, doctor, not in the normal sense of the word. It's just that the energy... well, it's just not there anymore. It's like a car that's run completely out of gas, just sitting in the driveway, and no matter how much you turn the key, the engine just won't catch.\n\nDoctor: During this time, was there ever a period of at least two weeks when the flatness and loss of interest were noticeably worse than usual and present most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Two weeks? Well... I couldn't rightly point to any specific two weeks on the calendar, doctor. You see, when you're just sitting in the house all day, the days just sort of blend into one another. I have this calendar hanging on the refrigerator—it’s one of those free ones they give you down at the pharmacy on Main Street, the one with the pictures of the national parks. October has a beautiful picture of the Grand Canyon, all those red and orange rocks, just lovely. I used to take a little red marker and put an 'X' through every day before I went to bed, just to keep track of things. But lately, I haven't even been doing that. The red marker is just sitting there in the little ceramic mug I keep my pens in. \n\nSo I don't really keep track of the weeks anymore. But as for it getting noticeably worse... no, I wouldn't say it ever dipped down worse than usual. It hasn't been like a rollercoaster, you know, going up and down and making your stomach drop. It’s just been one long, flat stretch. It actually reminds me of when my late husband Arthur and I drove out through the Midwest in our old Ford station wagon—it was powder blue with that fake wood paneling on the side, a real boat of a car. We drove through Nebraska, and it was just flat, flat, flat, as far as the eye could see, for hours on end. You'd look out the window and nothing ever changed. \n\nThat’s exactly how this feeling is. It’s just the same heavy, flat feeling every single day, from the time I wake up until I turn off my little fringed lamp at night. It doesn't get heavier, but it doesn't lift, either. So, no, I haven't had any particular two weeks where it took a sudden turn for the worse. Just the same steady flatness, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel this flat, unmotivated way even now, long after the retirement itself was over?\n\nPatient: Oh, yes, doctor. Very much so. It hasn't gone away at all, even though the retirement is well in the past now. Honestly... well, this whole thing, this heavy, flat feeling, it's been building since I retired from the school office. That was a little over two years ago now. \n\nI remember my last day so clearly. It was a Friday in late spring, and the principal, Mr. Henderson—such a tall man, he always wore those sweater vests with the little argyle patterns on the front—he brought in a sheet cake from the bakery down on 4th Street. It was vanilla with that whipped frosting, not the heavy buttercream, because he knew my stomach can't handle too much rich butter. They gave me a lovely little plaque, brass with my name engraved on it, thanking me for all my years keeping that front desk running and answering those ringing phones. I packed up my things, including that little ceramic mug I keep my pens in, put it all in a cardboard box, and I thought... well, I thought, now I’m going to have all this time to do my knitting and my church work and tend to my hydrangeas. I have the blue ones out front, you know, and you have to put coffee grounds in the soil to keep them that pretty color, otherwise they turn pink. \n\nBut... instead of doing all those things, I just sort of came home, set that cardboard box on the kitchen counter, and sat down in my chair. And it’s just been building ever since that very afternoon. A little over two years of just... sinking deeper into it. At first, I told myself I was just taking a few days to rest after all those years of working, but then the resting just never stopped, did it? The days turned into weeks, and the weeks into months, and here we are, two whole years later, and I’m still just feeling so terribly flat. It didn't happen all at once, like a light switch turning off. It just sort of crept up on me, building and building, day by day, since the moment I walked out of that school office for the last time.\n\nDoctor: Since retiring, have there been ongoing stresses in your life related to the retirement itself?\n\nPatient: Stresses? From the retirement? Well, no, not really. I mean, the retirement itself was quite straightforward. As I was saying, Mr. Henderson gave me that lovely plaque, and my pension paperwork was all sorted out by the district office weeks in advance. I had a very nice lady named Brenda help me with it—she had these beautiful, thick glasses with little rhinestones in the corners, very stylish for her age. She made sure all my forms were signed in triplicate, so the checks just started coming in the mail right on schedule, the first of every month, like clockwork. \n\nSo, financially, there hasn't been any stress. Arthur—bless his soul—he left me the house free and clear, so I don't have a mortgage to worry about. It’s a sturdy little brick ranch, built in the fifties, very solid bones. The only thing that ever gives me trouble is the gutters getting clogged with leaves from the big oak tree in the front yard, but the neighbor boy, Timmy, he comes over and cleans them out for a few dollars. He’s a good boy, always polite, always says 'Yes, ma'am' and 'No, ma'am.' \n\nSo, no, there hasn't been any stress about money or losing the house or anything like that. And I don't have to worry about getting up and fighting the traffic on Route 9 anymore, which used to be a terrible headache, especially when it rained and people forgot how to drive. It's just... quiet. The house is quiet, the street is quiet. There's no stress, doctor. There's just... nothing. Just me and the recliner and the clock ticking on the mantelpiece. It's an anniversary clock, the kind with the little gold balls that spin around at the bottom. Arthur bought it for our 25th wedding anniversary. It's very pretty, but when the house is this quiet, you can hear every single tick. Tick, tick, tick. But no, no stress. Just... just the quiet."
  }
